\displaystyle=\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}=E_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\ast\mathbf{b}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}=E_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\ast g\left(\mathbf{a}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)=g\left(E_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\ast\mathbf{a}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)=g(\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}})=g(\mathbf{a}),

so that
\rho\circ\rho^{\prime}=\id_{\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
, which concludes the proof of
(ii)
.
∎

Remark 20
.

Part
(ii)
of Lemma
19
does not hold in general if we relax the requirement that
\mathbf{A}
be
k
-enhanced. More precisely, in this case, the function
\rho:\Hom(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\to\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
defined in the proof of Lemma
19
still needs to be injective, but may not be surjective. Therefore, we have
\left|\Hom(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\right|\leq\left|\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\right|
, and the inequality may be strict.

For example, consider the Boolean structure
\mathbf{A}
having a unique unary relation
R_{1}^{\mathbf{A}}=A=\{0,1\}
. So,
\mathbf{A}
is
1
-enhanced but not
2
-enhanced. Observe that
|\Hom(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A})|=4
. The tensorised structure
\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}}
has domain
\{0,1\}^{2}=\{(0,0),(0,1),\allowbreak(1,0),(1,1)\}
, and its (unary) relation is
R_{1}^{\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}}}=\{0^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}},1^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}}\}=\{(0,0),(1,1)\}
. Therefore, each map
f:\{0,1\}^{2}\to\{0,1\}^{2}
such that
f((0,0))\in\{(0,0),(1,1)\}
and
f((1,1))\in\{(0,0),\allowbreak(1,1)\}
yields a proper homomorphism
\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}}\to\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}}
. Hence,
\left|\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}},\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}})\right|=64
, so
\Hom(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{A})
and
\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}},\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2}}}}})
are not in bijection.

5
Sherali-Adams acceptance

In this section, we connect the tensorisation construction to the Sherali-Adams hierarchy: We characterise the instances
\mathbf{X}
for which the
k
-th level of Sherali-Adams accepts as those
for which
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
is homomorphic to the free structure of the minion
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}
generated by
\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
, as stated in the following main result.

Theorem

(Theorem
7
restated)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, and let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures.
Then
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts if and only if

\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

The two implications of Theorem
7
are proved separately, in Propositions
21
and
22
. The “only if” implication, established in Proposition
22
, actually works for
k\geq 1
and does not require
k
-enhancement of the structures
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
. In any case, assuming
k
-enhancement results in no loss of generality, in the sense that, as noted in Section
3
, any
\PCSP
template is equivalent to the
\PCSP
template in which both structures are
k
-enhanced.
Subsequently, we show how certain known facts and new simple results on the Sherali-Adams hierarchy naturally follow from the geometry of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
through this characterisation.

Given two sets
S,T
, an integer
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and two tuples
\textbf{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{p})\in S^{p},\textbf{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\in T^{p}
such that
\textbf{s}\prec\textbf{t}
, we shall consider the function
f_{\textbf{s},\textbf{t}}:\{\textbf{s}\}\to T
defined by
f_{\textbf{s},\textbf{t}}(s_{\alpha})=t_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in[p]
.

Proposition 21
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures, and let
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a homomorphism. Then
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts.

Proof.

Given
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in X^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in A^{k}
such that
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}
, we define

\displaystyle\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})\coloneqq E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}).

(7)

Clearly, any pair
(V,f)
with
V\subseteq X
,
1\leq|V|\leq k
, and
f:V\to A
can be written as
(\{\mathbf{x}\},f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})
for some such tuples
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{a}
. We claim that the assignment (
7
) is consistent; i.e., that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
whenever
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in X^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{k})\in A^{k}
are such that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\prec\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
,
\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\}=\{\mathbf{x}\}
, and
f_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}
. From
\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\}=\{\mathbf{x}\}
, it follows that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
for some
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[k]^{k}
. From
f_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}=f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}
, it follows that, for any
\alpha\in[k]
,

\displaystyle a_{i_{\alpha}}=f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}(x_{i_{\alpha}})=f_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(x_{i_{\alpha}})=f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(x_{i_{\alpha}})=a^{\prime}_{\alpha},

so that
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Then, the claim can be rewritten as
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})
, which directly follows from Lemma
18
.

Consider now
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
,
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
,
\mathbf{a}\in A^{r}
such that
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}
. Let
m=|R^{\mathbf{A}}|
. Since
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
, we have
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
; being
\xi
a homomorphism, this implies that
\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
– i.e., there exists
q^{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(m)}
such that
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\xi(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})={q^{\mathbf{x}}}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. We define

\displaystyle\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q^{\mathbf{x}}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Clearly, any function
f:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A
can be (uniquely) written as
f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}
for some tuple
\mathbf{a}
. We claim that (
7
) and (
5
) constitute a proper assignment for
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
as defined in Section
2
.

To check (SA1), let
V=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}\subseteq X
, and let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
.

\displaystyle\sum_{f:V\to A}\lambda_{V}(f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=1,

where the third equality follows from Lemma
15
.

To check (SA2), consider
U\subseteq V\subseteq X
such that
1\leq|V|\leq k
and
f:U\to A
. We can write
V=\{\mathbf{x}\}
for some
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
,
U=\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}
for some
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
, and
f=f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}}
for some
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{a}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g:V\to A\\
g|_{U}=f\end{subarray}}\lambda_{V}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}})=\lambda_{U}(f),

where the third, fourth, and fifth equalities come from Lemma
15
, Lemma
17
, and Proposition
9
, respectively.

To check (SA3), consider
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
,
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
,
U\subseteq\{\mathbf{x}\}
such that
1\leq|U|\leq k
, and
f:U\to A
. Write
U=\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}
for some
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
and
f=f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}}
for some
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{a}
.

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A\\
g|_{U}=f\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{r}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q^{\mathbf{x}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q^{\mathbf{x}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q^{\mathbf{x}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast(q^{\mathbf{x}}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}})=\lambda_{U}(f),

where the third and fourth equalities come from Lemma
16
and Lemma
13
, respectively.
Finally, (SA4) directly follows from (
5
).

By the definition of the minion
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}
, both (
7
) and (
5
) assign rational values in the interval
[0,1]
to the variables. We conclude that
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts.
∎

Proposition 22
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
\sigma
-structures, and suppose that
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts. Then
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

Proof.

Consider a rational solution of
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
expressed in the notation of Section
2
, and let
\xi:X^{k}\to\mathcal{T}^{k;n}(\mathbb{Q})
be the function defined by

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})&\mbox{if }\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\mathbf{x}\in X^{k},\forall\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}.

Observe that
\xi(\mathbf{x})\geq 0
entrywise, and

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})=\sum_{f:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A}\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}\}}(f)=1

by (SA1). Therefore,
\xi(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(n^{k})}
. Take
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
. We need to show that
\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Let
m=|R^{\mathbf{A}}|
, and take
q\in\mathcal{T}^{1;m}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})&\mbox{if }\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}.

Observe that
q\geq 0
entrywise, and

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{r}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}})=\sum_{g:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(g)=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A\\
g(x_{1})=a\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\lambda_{\{x_{1}\}}(f_{x_{1},a})=1,

where the second, fifth, and sixth equalities come from (SA4), (SA3), and (SA1), respectively. It follows that
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(m)}
. We claim that
q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. For
\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}}).

(12)

For each
\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
satisfying
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}
, we have
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Hence, if
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\prec\mathbf{a}
, then
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=0
. From (
5
), in this case, we also have that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=0
. Suppose now that
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{a}
. Then, (
12
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{r}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{b}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g:\{\mathbf{x}\}\to A\\
g|_{\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}}=f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(g)=\lambda_{\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}}(f_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{a}})=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}),

where the second and fourth equalities come from (SA4) and (SA3), respectively. We conclude that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})
in all cases, so that
q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})
, as claimed. It follows that
\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
, and hence
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is a homomorphism.
∎

Remark 23
.

The characterisation of Sherali-Adams acceptance expressed in Theorem
7
can be straightforwardly adapted to yield a characterisation of acceptance for the (less powerful)
k
-consistency algorithm. Following
[
10
]
, the
k
-consistency algorithm applied to two
\sigma
-structures
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
accepts if and only if there exists a nonempty collection
\mathcal{F}
of partial homomorphisms from
\mathbf{X}
to
\mathbf{A}
with at most
k
-element domains such that
(i)

\mathcal{F}
is closed under restrictions, i.e., for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
and every
V\subseteq\dom(f)
,
f|_{V}\in\mathcal{F}
, and
(ii)

\mathcal{F}
has the extension property up to
k
, i.e., for every
f\in\mathcal{F}
and every
V\subseteq X
with
|V|\leq k
and
\dom(f)\subseteq V
, there exists
g\in\mathcal{F}
such that
g
extends
f
and
\dom(g)=V
. The role of the minion
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}
is now taken by the minion
\mathscr{H}=\Pol(\operatorname{HORN-3-SAT})
, whose
L
-ary elements are all nonempty subsets of
[L]
(cf.
[
10
]
) and whose free structure is the so-called power structure introduced in
[
55
]
(see also
[
39
]
).
The minion
\mathscr{H}
is closely related to
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}
: For each
L\in\mathbb{N}
, we can view
\mathscr{H}^{(L)}
as the set
\{\supp(q):q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(L)}\}
; given
h=\supp(q)\in\mathscr{H}^{(L)}
and
\pi:[L]\to[L^{\prime}]
,
h_{/\pi}=\supp(q_{/\pi})
. It follows that the results in Section
4
can be suitably modified to fit the minion
\mathscr{H}
. As a result, one easily adapts the proof of Proposition
21
to show that, if
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{H}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is a homomorphism,
the collection
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}:\mathbf{x}\in X^{k},\mathbf{a}\in\supp(\xi(\mathbf{x}))\}
witnesses that the
k
-consistency algorithm accepts. Conversely, again using
\mathscr{H}
-analogues of the results in Section
4
, the proof of Proposition
22
can be adapted to show that, given a collection
\mathcal{F}
of partial homomorphisms witnessing that the
k
-consistency algorithm accepts, the map
\xi:X^{k}\to\mathcal{T}^{k;n}(\{0,1\})
defined by setting, for
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
and
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=1
if
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{a}
and
f_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{a}}\in\mathcal{F}
,
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=0
otherwise yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{H}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.
We obtain the following fact:

Let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq\max(2,\max_{R\in\sigma}\ar(R))
.
Then, the
k
-consistency algorithm applied to
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
accepts if and only if
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{H}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.
10
10

10

Unlike in Theorem
7
, here we require that
k
is at least the maximum arity. This is an unsubstantial technicality due to this specific definition of
k
-consistency. Essentially, the reason is that any constraint whose scope has more than
k
distinct variables does not appear among the constraints of the partial homomorphisms witnessing
k
-consistency, while it does appear in the requirement (SA3) of Sherali-Adams.

The results presented in the remaining part of this section show how the multilinear nature of
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
helps describing the functioning of the Sherali-Adams hierarchy.

Proposition 24
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathbf{A}
be a
\sigma
-structure. Then
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
.

Proof.

If
k=1
the result is trivial (cf. Footnote
4
), so we assume
k\geq 2
. Let
J\in\mathcal{T}^{k-1;n}(\mathbb{Q})
be the all-one tensor, and consider, for each
j\in[k]
, some tuple
\bm{\ell}^{(j)}\in[k]^{k}
whose
k
-th entry is
j
.
Recalling the definition in (
3
), observe that the map

\displaystyle\psi:\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(n^{k})}

\displaystyle\to[\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(n)}]^{k}

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto(J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(1)}}\ast T,\dots,J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(k)}}\ast T)

is well defined since, for each
j\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in{A}}\mathbf{e}_{a}\ast(J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast T=\sum_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in{A}^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})(E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast T)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in{A}^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast T=1.

We claim that
\psi
is a homomorphism from
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
to
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
. To prove the claim, consider a relation symbol
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and take
M=[M_{\mathbf{i}}]_{\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}}\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. By the definition of free structure, we know that there exists a tuple
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
M_{\mathbf{i}}=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
, where
P_{\mathbf{i}}
is the tensor defined by (
3
). Consider now the tuple
\mathbf{v}=(P_{1}\ast q,\dots,P_{r}\ast q)
, where, for
j\in[r]
,
P_{j}
is the
|A|\times|R^{\mathbf{A}}|
matrix defined by

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{a}\ast P_{j}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }b_{j}=a\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 56.9055pt\forall a\in{A},\forall\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}.

Again by the definition of free structure (cf. Example
3
), we see that
\mathbf{v}\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})}
; hence,
\mathbf{v}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
. The claim is proved if we show that
\psi(M)=\mathbf{v}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
– or, equivalently, that
\psi(M_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}}
for each
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[r]^{k}
. First, notice that

\displaystyle\psi(M_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle=\psi(P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q)=(J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(1)}}\ast(P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q),\dots,J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(k)}}\ast(P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q))

\displaystyle=((J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(1)}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny k}}}{\ast}}P_{\mathbf{i}})\ast q,\dots,(J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(k)}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny k}}}{\ast}}P_{\mathbf{i}})\ast q),

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle=(P_{i_{1}}\ast q,\dots,P_{i_{k}}\ast q).

Therefore, to prove the claim, it suffices to argue that
J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny k}}}{\ast}}P_{\mathbf{i}}=P_{i_{j}}
for each
j\in[k]
. For
a\in{A}
and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, we find

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{a}\ast(J\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny k}}}{\ast}}P_{\mathbf{i}})\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}\\
a_{k}=a\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast(P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}\\
a_{k}=a\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in{A}^{k}}(E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})(E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{k}\\
a_{k}=a\end{subarray}}(E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\bm{\ell}^{(j)}}\ast E_{\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }b_{i_{j}}=a\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.=\mathbf{e}_{a}\ast P_{i_{j}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}},

where the second-to-last equality is due to the fact that the
k
-th entry of the tuple
\bm{\ell}^{(j)}
is
j
. Hence, the claim is true.
∎

Combining Proposition
24
with Proposition
22
, one finds that, if
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}.

By Lemma
19
, this yields
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
, which implies that
\BLP(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts (cf. Appendix
A
). Hence, Proposition
24
reflects the well-known fact that any level of Sherali-Adams is at least as powerful as
\BLP
(see also Remark
38
). In a similar way, the next proposition is the geometric counterpart of the algorithmic fact that higher levels of Sherali-Adams are tighter than lower levels.

Proposition 25
.

Let
k\geq p\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be
\sigma
-structures, and suppose that
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Then
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}})
.

Proof.

Fix a homomorphism
f:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, and consider the function
g:X^{p}\to\mathcal{T}^{p;n}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by
(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\mapsto f((x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{1},x_{1},\dots,x_{1}))\ast J
, where
J\in\mathcal{T}^{k-p;n}(\mathbb{Q})
is the all-one tensor. Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{p}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g((x_{1},\dots,x_{p}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{p}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast f((x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{1},\dots,x_{1}))\ast J

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{p}}\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast f((x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{1},\dots,x_{1}))\ast E_{\mathbf{b}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{c}\in{A}^{k}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast f((x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{1},\dots,x_{1}))=1.

We now show that
g
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}})
. Take
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
, so
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}}}
. We need to show that
g(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny p}}}}})}
. Observe that
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
; since
f
is a homomorphism, we have
f(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Therefore, there exists
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
f(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. Take
\bm{\ell}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{p})\in[r]^{p}
. We claim that
g(\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\ell}})=q_{/\pi_{\bm{\ell}}}
. Consider
\mathbf{j}\coloneqq(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{p},\ell_{1},\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{1})\in[r]^{k}
. For
\mathbf{a}\in{A}^{p}
, we have

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g(\mathbf{x}_{\bm{\ell}})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast f((x_{\ell_{1}},\dots,x_{\ell_{p}},x_{\ell_{1}},\dots,x_{\ell_{1}}))\ast J=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast f(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{j}})\ast J

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast J=\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast E_{\mathbf{b}}=\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}E_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}E_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast q=\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}}(E_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast q)\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k-p}}(E_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b})}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{\bm{\ell}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast q

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}}(E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\bm{\ell}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}})(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast q)=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\bm{\ell}}\ast q=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\bm{\ell}}}.

This shows that the claim is true, thus concluding the proof of the proposition.
∎

The following result will be used in Section
6
(specifically, in the proof of Proposition
12
). It reflects the fact that Sherali-Adams solutions only give a nonzero weight to those assignments that yield partial homomorphisms from
\mathbf{X}
to
\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 26
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures, and let
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a homomorphism. Let
R\in\sigma
have arity
r
, and take
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
,
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
, and
\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{r}
. If
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, then
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=0
.

Proof.

From
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
we have
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
and, thus,
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. It follows that there exists
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}
for each
\mathbf{j}\in[r]^{k}
. Proposition
9
then yields

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\xi(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}=P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast q.

(13)

Consider, for each
\alpha\in[k]
, the set
S_{\alpha}=\{\beta\in[r]:i_{\beta}=\alpha\}
, and fix an element
\hat{\beta}\in[r]
. The tuple
\mathbf{j}\in[r]^{k}
defined by setting
j_{\alpha}=\min S_{\alpha}
if
S_{\alpha}\neq\emptyset
,
j_{\alpha}=\hat{\beta}
otherwise satisfies
\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{i}}}=\mathbf{i}
. We obtain

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast q

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{b}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q,

(14)

where the first equality comes from Lemma
13
and the second from Lemma
16
. We claim that the sum on the right-hand side of (
14
) is zero. Indeed, let
\mathbf{b}\in A^{r}
satisfy
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\prec\mathbf{b}
and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}
. For any
\alpha\in[r]
we have
i_{\alpha}=i_{j_{i_{\alpha}}}
, so
x_{i_{\alpha}}=x_{i_{j_{i_{\alpha}}}}
and, hence,
b_{\alpha}=b_{j_{i_{\alpha}}}
. It follows that
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{i}}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}_{\mathbf{i}}}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, which proves the claim. Combining this with (
13
), (
14
), and Lemma
17
, we find

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast q=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{i}_{\mathbf{j}}}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x})\geq E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

The next result – in combination with Theorem
7
– yields a geometric explanation for the known fact that, for every
\PCSP
template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
, the
k
-th level of Sherali-Adams correctly classifies any instance
\mathbf{X}
of
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
such that the domain of
\mathbf{X}
contains
k
(or fewer) elements.

Proposition 27
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures such that
X=[k]
, and let
g:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
be a homomorphism. Given
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\supp(g((1,\dots,k)))
, the function
f:X\to A
defined by
j\mapsto a_{j}
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}
to
\mathbf{A}
.

Proof.

Let
R\in\sigma
be a relation symbol of arity
r
, and take a tuple
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
. Notice that
f(\mathbf{x})=(a_{x_{1}},\dots,a_{x_{r}})=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}}
. For the sake of contradiction, suppose that
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}}\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. Since
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
, we have
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
; being
g
a homomorphism, this implies that
g(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Therefore,
\exists q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. Set
\mathbf{y}\coloneqq\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\in[k]^{k}
, and observe that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast g(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{c}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}\\
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{c}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{c}}\ast q,

where the fourth and fifth equalities comes from Lemma
13
and Lemma
16
, respectively. On the other hand, Proposition
9
and Lemma
17
yield

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast g(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast g((1,\dots,k)_{\mathbf{y}})=E_{\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{y}}\ast g((1,\dots,k))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in{A}^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{y}}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast g((1,\dots,k))\geq E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g((1,\dots,k))>0.

We deduce that, for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
, there exists some
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
such that
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}
and
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{c}
. Choose
\mathbf{i}
so that
\{\mathbf{x}\}=\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}
. Since
\mathbf{c}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
and
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}}\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, we have
\mathbf{c}\neq\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}}
; so,
c_{p}\neq a_{x_{p}}
for some
p\in[r]
. From
x_{p}\in\{\mathbf{x}\}=\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}
, we obtain
x_{p}=x_{i_{t}}
for some
t\in[k]
. Since
\mathbf{x}\prec\mathbf{c}
, this yields
c_{p}=c_{i_{t}}
. Therefore,
c_{i_{t}}=c_{p}\neq a_{x_{p}}=a_{x_{i_{t}}}
, so
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{i}}\neq\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}
, a contradiction. We conclude that
f(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{x}}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, so that
f
is a homomorphism.
∎

Remark 28
.

Proposition
27
is not true if the requirement that
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
be
k
-enhanced is dropped. For example, let
k=3
, and suppose that
\mathbf{X}
is a directed
3
-cycle on domain
[3]
and
\mathbf{A}
is
\mathbf{K}_{2}
. Consider the function
g:[3]^{3}\to\mathcal{T}^{3;2}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by

\begin{array}[]{lllllll}&g((1,1,1))=g((2,2,2))=g((3,3,3))&=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}cc|cc@{}}\frac{1}{2}&0&0&0\\
0&0&0&\frac{1}{2}\end{array}\right]\\[10.0pt]
&g((1,1,2))=g((2,2,1))=g((2,2,3))=g((3,3,2))=g((3,3,1))=g((1,1,3))&=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}cc|cc@{}}0&\frac{1}{2}&0&0\\
0&0&\frac{1}{2}&0\end{array}\right]\\[10.0pt]
&g((1,2,2))=g((2,1,1))=g((2,3,3))=g((3,2,2))=g((3,1,1))=g((1,3,3))&=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}cc|cc@{}}0&0&\frac{1}{2}&0\\
0&\frac{1}{2}&0&0\end{array}\right]\\[10.0pt]
&g((1,2,1))=g((2,1,2))=g((2,3,2))=g((3,2,3))=g((3,1,3))=g((1,3,1))&=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}cc|cc@{}}0&0&0&\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1}{2}&0&0&0\end{array}\right]\\[10.0pt]
&g((1,2,3))=g((1,3,2))=g((2,1,3))=g((2,3,1))=g((3,1,2))=g((3,2,1))&=\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}cc|cc@{}}\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}\\
\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}&\frac{1}{8}\end{array}\right].\end{array}

One can check that
g
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 3}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 3}}}}})
. Observe that
\supp(g((1,2,3)))=[2]^{3}
, so Proposition
27
would imply that any map
[3]\to[2]
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}
to
\mathbf{A}
– which is certainly false, as
\mathbf{X}\not\to\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition
27
is also not true if the requirement
k\geq 2
is dropped. For example, consider the
1
-enhanced structures
\mathbf{X}=([1];R_{1}^{\mathbf{X}}=[1],R^{\mathbf{X}}=\{(1,1)\})
and
\mathbf{A}=([2];R_{1}^{\mathbf{A}}=[2],R^{\mathbf{A}}=\{(1,2),(2,1)\})
. The assignment
1\mapsto(0.5,0.5)
yields a homomorphism
g:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
with
\supp(g(1))=[2]
. However,
\mathbf{X}\not\to\mathbf{A}
.

We now present an interesting application of the tensorisation machinery to the problem of counting homomorphisms: The number of homomorphisms between two
\sigma
-structures
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
can be read off on the image of a specific tuple under a maximum-support homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
for suitable
k
.

Given two homomorphisms
g_{A},g_{B}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
, one easily checks that the assignment
x\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(g_{A}(x)+g_{B}(x))
yields a new homomorphism
g_{C}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
. As a consequence, if
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
, there exists some homomorphism
g:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
having
maximum support
– i.e., such that
\supp(g(x))\supseteq\supp(g^{\prime}(x))
for any
g^{\prime}:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
and for any
x\in X
. Such homomorphism can be found in polynomial time (in the size of
\mathbf{X}
) by searching a relative-interior point in the polytope described by the constraints of
\BLP(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})

[
83
]
.

Proposition 29
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, and let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be two
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structures such that
X=[k]
. Then

•

|\Hom(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})|=0
if
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\not\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
;

•

|\Hom(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})|=|\supp(g((1,\dots,k)))|
if
g:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
has maximum support.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\not\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, by Proposition
22

\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
does not accept. Hence,
\mathbf{X}\not\to\mathbf{A}
, so that
\Hom(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})=\emptyset
.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
and
g
is a homomorphism having maximum support. As a consequence of Proposition
27
, we can build the map

\displaystyle m:\supp(g((1,\dots,k)))

\displaystyle\to\Hom(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{\mathbf{a}}

where
f_{\mathbf{a}}
is defined by
j\mapsto a_{j}
. Clearly,
m
is injective. Given a homomorphism
f:\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{A}
, consider the map
g_{f}:X^{k}\to\mathcal{T}^{k;n}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by
\mathbf{x}\mapsto E_{f(\mathbf{x})}
. We claim that
g_{f}
is a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Indeed, take
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
and take
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
, so that
\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
. Since
f
is a homomorphism,
f(\mathbf{x})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
; consider then
q\coloneqq\mathbf{e}_{f(\mathbf{x})}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
. For any
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
,
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in{A}^{k}
, observe that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{f(\mathbf{x})}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{if }f(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast E_{f(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})}=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast g_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}),

so that
g_{f}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\forall\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
, which means that
g_{f}(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Therefore, the claim is true.

Let
\mathbf{a}\coloneqq(f(1),\dots,f(k))\in{A}^{k}
, and observe that
\mathbf{a}\in\supp(g_{f}((1,\dots,k)))\subseteq\supp(g((1,\dots,k)))
. For any
j\in X
, we have
[m(\mathbf{a})](j)=f_{\mathbf{a}}(j)=f(j)
, so
f=m(\mathbf{a})
. We deduce that
m
is surjective, too, whence the result follows.
∎

The next proposition establishes that symmetries of
\mathbf{A}
can be lifted to symmetries of the solutions of
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
. As a result, one can always find a solution of
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
that is, in some sense, at least as symmetric as
\mathbf{A}
. We let
\Aut(\mathbf{A})
denote the group of automorphisms of
\mathbf{A}
(where an
automorphism
is a bijective homomorphism from
\mathbf{A}
to itself whose inverse is also a homomorphism).

Proposition 30
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be
\sigma
-structures, and suppose that
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Then there exists a homomorphism
\bar{g}:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
having the following property:
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\bar{g}(\mathbf{x})=E_{\vartheta(\mathbf{a})}\ast\bar{g}(\mathbf{x})
for each
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k},\mathbf{a}\in A^{k},\vartheta\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Choose a homomorphism
g:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
, and let
\tau
be an automorphism of
\mathbf{A}
. Consider the map
g^{\tau}:X^{k}\to\mathcal{T}^{k;n}(\mathbb{Q})
defined by
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast g(\mathbf{x})
for each
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k},\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast g(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g(\mathbf{x})=1

for any
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
, so
g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(n^{k})}
. We claim that
g^{\tau}
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Let
R\in\sigma
be a relation symbol of arity
r
, and let
\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
. Since
g
is a homomorphism,
g(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
, so there exists some
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. Using that
\tau
is an automorphism of
\mathbf{A}
, we can define the tuple
q^{\tau}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
by setting
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q^{\tau}\coloneqq\mathbf{e}_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{b})}\ast q
for each
\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. We claim that
g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})=q^{\tau}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
; this would imply that
g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Given
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
and using Lemma
13
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllllllllll}&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q^{\tau}_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}&=&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q^{\tau}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q^{\tau}&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{b})}\ast q\\[30.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
(\tau(\mathbf{b}))_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\tau(\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}})=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q\end{array}

and

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllllllllll}&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})&=&\displaystyle E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})&=&\displaystyle E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}&=&\displaystyle E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q\\[10.0pt]
&&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast q,\end{array}

thus proving the claims.

We have shown that
g^{\tau}
is a homomorphism from
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
for each
\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})
. Clearly, any convex combination of such homomorphisms is still a homomorphism (since LP solutions are closed under convex combinations). It follows that

\displaystyle\bar{g}\coloneqq\frac{1}{|\Aut(\mathbf{A})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})}g^{\tau}

is a homomorphism, too.
Given
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
,
\mathbf{a}\in A^{k}
, and
\vartheta\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\vartheta(\mathbf{a})}\ast\bar{g}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Aut(\mathbf{A})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})}E_{\vartheta(\mathbf{a})}\ast g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Aut(\mathbf{A})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})}E_{(\tau^{-1}\circ\vartheta)(\mathbf{a})}\ast g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Aut(\mathbf{A})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})}E_{\tau^{-1}(\mathbf{a})}\ast g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Aut(\mathbf{A})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Aut(\mathbf{A})}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g^{\tau}(\mathbf{x})=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\bar{g}(\mathbf{x}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

We shall notice, in Section
6
, that the explicit homomorphism
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
whose existence proves Proposition
11
is invariant under the automorphism group of the clique – that is, under the symmetric group. In other words, that homomorphism could be indicated by
\bar{g}
in the language of Proposition
30
.

6
Sherali-Adams and graph colouring

In this section, we use the tensorisation machinery developed in Sections
4
and
5
to prove the following main result.

Theorem

(Theorem
10
restated)
.

No constant level of Sherali-Adams solves the approximate graph colouring problem;
i.e., for any fixed
3\leq c\leq d
, there is no constant
k
such that the
k
-th level of Sherali-Adams solves
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
.

The key step towards the proof of Theorem
10
is Proposition
11
– which, in turn, relies on the following combinatorial fact. Recall that, for two tuples
s
and
t
of equal length, the notation
\textbf{s}\sim\textbf{t}
means that
\textbf{s}\prec\textbf{t}
and
\textbf{t}\prec\textbf{s}
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
c,k\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider the tuples
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}\in[k]^{k}
,
\mathbf{x}\in[c]^{k}
. Then

\displaystyle|\{\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}:\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\mbox{ and }\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we shall assume that
c\geq k
. Let
S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
denote the set
\{\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}:\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\mbox{ and }\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}
, and let
s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
denote the cardinality of
S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
.

Suppose first that
\mathbf{a}\not\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. In this case, if
\mathbf{b}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
, then
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}
, which implies
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Since
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}
, this yields
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
, a contradiction. Hence,
s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}=0
as required.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. We use induction on the number
k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|
. If
k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|=0
, the elements in the tuple
\mathbf{x}
are all distinct. Hence,
S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
is the set of tuples
\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}
such that all the elements of
\mathbf{b}
are distinct and
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
. This amounts to assigning distinct values from
[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{a}\}
to each
b_{i}
with
i\in[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{i}\}
. Observing that
|\{\mathbf{i}\}|=|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|=|\{\mathbf{a}\}|
, this results in
(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!
choices, so the result holds in this case. Suppose now, for the inductive step, that
k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|>0
. Take
v,w\in[k]
such that
v\neq w
and
x_{v}=x_{w}
. From
|\{\mathbf{x}\}|<k\leq c
we have that
\{\mathbf{x}\}\subsetneqq[c]
, so we can find an element
p\in[c]\setminus\{\mathbf{x}\}
. Consider the tuple
{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}\in[c]^{k}
defined by
{x}^{\prime}_{\alpha}=x_{\alpha}
if
\alpha\neq w
,
{x}^{\prime}_{w}=p
. Consider also the tuple
{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}\in[k]^{k}
defined by
{i}^{\prime}_{\beta}=i_{\beta}
if
i_{\beta}\neq w
,
{i}^{\prime}_{\beta}=v
otherwise. We claim that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}
. Indeed, for any
\beta\in[k]
, if
i_{\beta}\neq w
we have
x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=x^{\prime}_{i_{\beta}}=x_{i_{\beta}}
, while if
i_{\beta}=w
we have
x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=x^{\prime}_{v}=x_{v}=x_{w}=x_{i_{\beta}}
. It follows that
\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}
. Consider now a tuple
\mathbf{b}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
. For any
r\in[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}
we can consider a new tuple
\mathbf{b}^{(r)}\in[k]^{k}
defined by
b^{(r)}_{\alpha}=b_{\alpha}
if
\alpha\neq w
,
b^{(r)}_{w}=r
.
We claim that

\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=\{\mathbf{b}^{(r)}:\mathbf{b}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}\mbox{ and }r\in[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}\}.

(15)

To prove the
‘
\supseteq
’
inclusion, observe first that
\mathbf{b}^{(r)}\sim\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
follows from the fact that
r\not\in\{\mathbf{b}\}
and
p\not\in\{\mathbf{x}\}
. We now need to show that
\mathbf{b}^{(r)}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{a}
. Take
\beta\in[k]
. If
i_{\beta}\neq w
,
b^{(r)}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=b^{(r)}_{i_{\beta}}=b_{i_{\beta}}=a_{\beta}
. If
i_{\beta}=w
,
b^{(r)}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=b^{(r)}_{v}=b_{v}
. From
\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}
and
x_{v}=x_{w}
, it follows
b_{v}=b_{w}
, whence
b^{(r)}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=b_{w}=b_{i_{\beta}}=a_{\beta}
. This concludes the proof that
\mathbf{b}^{(r)}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
. To prove the
‘
\subseteq
’
inclusion, take
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}
, let
r=b^{\prime}_{w}
, and consider the tuple
\mathbf{d}\in[k]^{k}
defined by
d_{\alpha}=b^{\prime}_{\alpha}
if
\alpha\neq w
,
d_{w}=b^{\prime}_{v}
. We claim that
r\in[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{d}\}
. Indeed, if
r\in\{\mathbf{d}\}
, then
r=b^{\prime}_{t}
for some
t\in[k]
,
t\neq w
. But then
b^{\prime}_{t}=b^{\prime}_{w}
, and from
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\sim\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
we would get
x^{\prime}_{t}=x^{\prime}_{w}
, whence
x_{t}=x^{\prime}_{t}=x^{\prime}_{w}=p
; this is a contradiction, since
p\not\in\{\mathbf{x}\}
. Next, we claim that
\mathbf{d}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
. By the definition of
\mathbf{d}
, one readily checks that
\mathbf{d}\sim\mathbf{x}
. To show that
\mathbf{d}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
, take
\beta\in[k]
. If
i_{\beta}\neq w
,
d_{i_{\beta}}=b^{\prime}_{i_{\beta}}=b^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=a_{\beta}
, while, if
i_{\beta}=w
,
d_{i_{\beta}}=d_{w}=b^{\prime}_{v}=b^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}_{\beta}}=a_{\beta}
. It is also clear from the definition of
\mathbf{d}
that
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}^{(r)}
.
This concludes the proof of (
15
).

If
\mathbf{b}\in S_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}
, then
|[k]\setminus\{\mathbf{b}\}|=k-|\{\mathbf{b}\}|=k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|
. Therefore, it follows from (
15
) that
s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}\cdot(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)
.
Observe that
|\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\}|=|\{\mathbf{x}\}|+1
, so
k-|\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\}|=k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|-1
. Applying the inductive hypothesis, we find

\displaystyle s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|}\cdot s_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{i}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|}\cdot\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{i}^{\prime}}\}|)!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\}|)!}=\frac{1}{k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|}\cdot\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|-1)!}=\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}

as required.
∎

Proposition

(Proposition
11
restated)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a loopless digraph and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 2
. Then
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{k})
accepts.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}
) be obtained from
\mathbf{X}
(resp.
\mathbf{K}_{k}
) by adding the relation
R_{k}^{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}=X^{k}
(resp.
R_{k}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}=[k]^{k}
). Clearly,
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{k})
accepts if and only if
\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k})
accepts. By virtue of Theorem
7
, then, it is sufficient to show that
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.
For brevity, we denote
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
by
F
.

Consider the function

\displaystyle g:X^{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}^{k;k}(\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}{k!}G_{\mathbf{x}}

where
G_{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{T}^{k;k}(\mathbb{Q})
is the tensor defined by

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast G_{\mathbf{x}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\hskip 28.45274pt\forall\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}.

Notice that, for any
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast G_{\mathbf{x}}=|\{\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}:\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|=k(k-1)(k-2)\dots(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|+1)=\frac{k!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}.

It follows that
\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g(\mathbf{x})=1
, so
g(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(k^{k})}

\forall\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
. To conclude the proof, we need to show that
g
yields a homomorphism from
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
F
.

Let
{R}
be the binary relation symbol corresponding to the edge sets of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{K}_{k}
. Take an element of
{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
and write it as
(x,y)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
, where
(x,y)\in R^{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}
(so
x\neq y
, because
\mathbf{X}
is loopless). We need to show that
g((x,y)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in{R}^{\textbf{F}}
. Observe that
|{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}|=k^{2}-k
. Take
q\coloneqq\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}\mathbf{1}_{k^{2}-k}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}|)}
. We claim that
g((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\forall\mathbf{i}\in[2]^{k}
; this would imply that
g((x,y)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in{R}^{\textbf{F}}
. For
\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}
, observe that

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast q=\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{1}_{k^{2}-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}\sum_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{e}_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}|\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}:(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\}|,

(16)

where we have used (
3
).
Suppose now that
\mathbf{i}=(1,\dots,1)
. In this case, (
6
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}|\{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{R}^{\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}}:(x^{\prime},\dots,x^{\prime})=\mathbf{a}\}|=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{k}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\mbox{ is constant}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g((x,\dots,x))=\frac{1}{k}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast G_{(x,\dots,x)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{k}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\mbox{ is constant}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Hence, the claim holds in this case. The case
\mathbf{i}=(2,\dots,2)
follows analogously. Suppose now that
\mathbf{i}\not\in\{(1,\dots,1),(2,\dots,2)\}
. In this case, (
6
) yields

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{i}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g((x,y)_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast G_{(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{1}{k^{2}-k}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

so the claim holds in this case, too, since
\mathbf{i}\sim(x,y)_{\mathbf{i}}
and
‘
\sim
’
is transitive. It follows that
g
preserves
{R}
.

We now claim that
g
satisfies the consistency equation (
5
). Take
\mathbf{x}\in X^{k}
and
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{a}\in[k]^{k}
. Using Lemma
17
and Lemma
31
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{i}}\ast g(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast g(\mathbf{x})=\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast G_{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}{k!}|\{\mathbf{b}\in[k]^{k}:\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\mbox{ and }\mathbf{b}\sim\mathbf{x}\}|

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}{k!}\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}\}|)!}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{k!}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast g(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})

\displaystyle=\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{k!}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast G_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\frac{(k-|\{\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\}|)!}{k!}&\mbox{if }\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

so the claim holds. It follows by Proposition
9
that
g
preserves the
k
-ary symbol
R_{k}
. Hence,
g
is a homomorphism from
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
F
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 32
.

A direct consequence of Proposition
11
is that
\SA^{k}
does not solve
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
if
3\leq c\leq d
and
2\leq k\leq c
.
To see this, let us denote by
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
the structure obtained from a given structure
\mathbf{A}
by adding the relation
R_{k}^{\tilde{\mathbf{A}}}=A^{k}
. By Proposition
11
,
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{K}_{d+1},\mathbf{K}_{k})
(and thus
\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d+1},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k})
) accepts. By Theorem
7
,
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d+1}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Since
k\leq c
,
\mathbf{K}_{k}\to\mathbf{K}_{c}
, so
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}
, which implies by Lemma
19

\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
and by Lemma
41
in Appendix
B

\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{k}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Composing the two homomorphisms, we get
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d+1}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
so, by Theorem
7
,
\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d+1},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c})
(and thus
\SA^{k}({\mathbf{K}}_{d+1},{\mathbf{K}}_{c})
) accepts. However, clearly,
\mathbf{K}_{d+1}\not\to\mathbf{K}_{d}
.

Remark 33
.

A second consequence of Proposition
11
(combined with Proposition
27
) is the complete characterisation of the behaviour of Sherali-Adams on cliques: Letting
c,d,k\in\mathbb{N}
be at least
2
,
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
accepts if and only if
\min(k,c)\leq d
.

To show this, let us denote by
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
the structure obtained from a given structure
\mathbf{A}
by adding the relation
R_{t}^{\tilde{\mathbf{A}}}=A^{t}
for each
t\in[k]
.

If
k\leq d
, by Proposition
11

\SA^{d}(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
accepts, which implies that
\SA^{d}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d})
accepts whence, by Proposition
22
,
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny d}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny d}}}}})
. Applying Proposition
25
, we deduce that
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. From Theorem
7
, we find that
\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d})
accepts and, hence,
\SA^{k}({\mathbf{K}}_{c},{\mathbf{K}}_{d})
accepts.

If
c\leq d
, the following chain of implications holds:

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{d}\hskip 11.38092pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.38092pt\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}\\[2.0pt]
\Rightarrow&\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\\[2.0pt]
\Rightarrow&\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d})\mbox{ accepts}\hskip 11.38092pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.38092pt\SA^{k}({\mathbf{K}}_{c},{\mathbf{K}}_{d})\mbox{ accepts},\end{array}

where the first and fourth implications are trivial, the second follows from Lemma
19
and Lemma
40
in Appendix
B
, and the third from Theorem
7
.

If
m\coloneqq\min(k,c)>d
, suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
accepts, whence it follows that
\SA^{k}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c},\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d})
accepts. The following chain of implications holds:

\begin{array}[]{llll}&\mathbf{K}_{m}\to\mathbf{K}_{c}\hskip 11.38092pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.38092pt\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{m}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}\\[2.0pt]
\Rightarrow&\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{m}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\\[2.0pt]
\Rightarrow&\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{m}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny m}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny m}}}}})\\[2.0pt]
\Rightarrow&\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{m}\to\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}\hskip 11.38092pt\Rightarrow\hskip 11.38092pt\mathbf{K}_{m}\to\mathbf{K}_{d},\end{array}

where the first and fifth implications are trivial, the second follows from Lemma
19
and Theorem
7
, the third from Proposition
25
, and the fourth from Proposition
27
(notice that
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{m}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{d}
are
m
-enhanced). Since
m>d
, this leads to a contradiction.

We now use Proposition
11
to prove Theorem
10
in full generality by exploiting the properties of the line digraph construction. Let
\mathbf{X}
be a digraph, whose binary edge relation shall be denoted by
R^{\mathbf{X}}
. The
line digraph
of
\mathbf{X}
is the digraph
\delta\mathbf{X}
on vertex set
R^{\mathbf{X}}
whose binary relation is
S^{\delta\mathbf{X}}=\{((x,y),(y,z)):(x,y),(y,z)\in R^{\mathbf{X}}\}
.
The line digraph construction has been utilised in
[
90
,
60
]
as a polynomial-time (and in fact log-space) reduction between
\PCSP
s. In particular, the construction changes the chromatic number in a controlled way, as we now describe.

Consider the integer functions
a
and
b
defined by
a(p)=2^{p}
and
b(p)={p\choose\lfloor p/2\rfloor}
for
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and notice that
a(p)\geq b(p)
for each
p
. Let also
a^{(i)}
(resp.
b^{(i)}
) be the function obtained by iterating
a
(resp.
b
)
i
-many times, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. The following result bounds the chromatic number of the line digraph in terms of that of the original digraph.

Theorem 34

(
[
61
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a digraph and let
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

If
\delta\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{p}
, then
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{a(p)}
.

•

If
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{b(p)}
, then
\delta\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{p}
.

It was suggested by Jakub Opršal that the line digraph construction not only provides a polynomial-time reduction,
but also preserves acceptance by Sherali-Adams. We now prove this fact using the tensorisation machinery.

Proposition

(Proposition
12
restated)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq 2
, let
\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A}
be digraphs, and suppose that
\SA^{2k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts. Then
\SA^{k}(\delta\mathbf{X},\delta\mathbf{A})
accepts.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathbf{A}}
) be obtained from
\mathbf{X}
(resp.
\mathbf{A}
) by adding the relations
R_{t}^{\tilde{\mathbf{X}}}=X^{t}
(resp.
R_{t}^{\tilde{\mathbf{A}}}=A^{t}
) for each
t\in[2k]
. Let also
\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{X}}
(resp.
\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{A}}
) be obtained from
\delta\mathbf{X}
(resp.
\delta\mathbf{A}
) by adding the relation
R_{k}^{\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{X}}}=(R^{\mathbf{X}})^{k}
(resp.
R_{k}^{\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{A}}}=(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}
). Clearly,
\SA^{2k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts if and only if
\SA^{2k}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}},\tilde{\mathbf{A}})
accepts, and
\SA^{k}(\delta\mathbf{X},\delta\mathbf{A})
accepts if and only if
\SA^{k}(\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{X}},\widetilde{\delta\mathbf{A}})
accepts. To avoid cumbersome notation, we shall not write the symbols
\sim
explicitly. By virtue of Theorem
7
, there exists a homomorphism
\xi:\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}})
, and to prove the proposition we need to build a homomorphism
\vartheta:\delta\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\delta\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. Given a tuple of arcs
\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{k})\in(R^{\mathbf{X}})^{k}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{\ell}=(x_{\ell,1},x_{\ell,2})\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
for each
\ell\in[k]
, we shall consider the tuple of vertices
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(x_{1,1},x_{1,2},x_{2,1},x_{2,2},\dots,x_{k,1},x_{k,2})\in X^{2k}
; given
\mathbf{a}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}
, we define
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in A^{2k}
analogously. We set

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}).

Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}}E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\leq\sum_{\mathbf{b}\in A^{2k}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=1.

(17)

If (
17
) hold with strict inequality, we would have that
E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})>0
for some
\mathbf{b}\in A^{2k}
such that
(b_{2i-1},b_{2i})\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
for some
i\in[k]
. Taking
\mathbf{i}=(2i-1,2i)\in[2k]^{2}
, this means that
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, while, by definition of
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{i}}\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
. Applying Proposition
26
, we get a contradiction. It follows that
\vartheta(\mathbf{x})\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(m^{k})}
, where
m=|R^{\mathbf{A}}|
. We claim that
\vartheta
yields a homomorphism from
\delta\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\delta\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
.

To show that
\vartheta
preserves the binary symbol
S
, take
((x,y),(y,z))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{X}}
, so
((x,y),(y,z))^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in S^{\delta\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
. We need to check that
\vartheta(((x,y),(y,z))^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\in S^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\delta\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})}
. Observe that
(x,y,z)\in X^{3}=R_{3}^{\mathbf{X}}
, so
(x,y,z)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}}\in R_{3}^{\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}}}
and, hence,
\xi((x,y,z)^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}})\in R_{3}^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny 2k}}}}})}
. It follows that there exists
w\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(|R_{3}^{\mathbf{A}}|)}=\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(n^{3})}
such that
\xi((x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}})=w_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}
for each
\mathbf{i}\in[3]^{2k}
. Let
\ell\coloneqq|S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}|
, and consider the rational
\ell
-vector
q
defined by
\mathbf{e}_{((a,b),(b,c))}\ast q\coloneqq\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w
for
((a,b),(b,c))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}
. We claim that
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(\ell)}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\sum_{((a,b),(b,c))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}}\mathbf{e}_{((a,b),(b,c))}\ast q

\displaystyle=\sum_{((a,b),(b,c))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}}\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w\leq\sum_{(a,b,c)\in A^{3}}\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w=1.

(18)

Suppose (
18
) holds with strict inequality. It follows that
\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w>0
for some
(a,b,c)\in A^{3}
such that
(a,b)\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
or
(b,c)\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. Assume
(a,b)\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
