(the other case follows analogously). Consider the tuples
\mathbf{i}=(1,2,3,3,\dots,3)\in[3]^{2k}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a,b,c,c,\dots,c)\in A^{2k}
, and
\mathbf{j}=(1,2)\in[2k]^{2}
. Note that
((x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{j}}=(x,y)\in R^{\mathbf{X}}
while
\mathbf{a}_{\mathbf{j}}=(a,b)\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. It follows from Proposition
26
that
E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi((x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}})=0
, whence we find

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\xi((x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}})=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast w_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast w=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in A^{3}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{b}}\ast w=\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w,

a contradiction (where we have used Lemma
13
). We deduce that (
18
) holds with equality, so
q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}^{(\ell)}
as claimed. Consider now
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})\in[2]^{k}
, and set
\mathbf{x}=((x,y),(y,z))_{\mathbf{j}}\in(R^{\mathbf{X}})^{k}
. We claim that
\vartheta(\mathbf{x})=q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}
. This would conclude the proof that
\vartheta
preserves
S
. For
\mathbf{a}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}
, we find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast P_{\mathbf{j}}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}((a,b),(b,c))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}\\
((a,b),(b,c))_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{((a,b),(b,c))}\ast q=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}((a,b),(b,c))\in S^{\delta\mathbf{A}}\\
((a,b),(b,c))_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w.

(19)

Consider the tuple
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{2k})\in[3]^{2k}
defined by
i_{2\ell-1}=j_{\ell}
,
i_{2\ell}=j_{\ell}+1
for each
\ell\in[k]
. Observe that this choice guarantees
(x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi((x,y,z)_{\mathbf{i}})=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast w_{/\pi_{\mathbf{i}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast P_{\mathbf{i}}\ast w=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a,b,c)\in A^{3}\\
(a,b,c)_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w.

(20)

Note that, given
(a,b),(b,c)\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
,
((a,b),(b,c))_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}
if and only if
(a,b,c)_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
. Hence, combining (
19
) and (
20
) yields

E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}\leq E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})
,
and strict inequality can happen only if there exists a tuple
(a,b,c)\in A^{3}
such that
\mathbf{e}_{(a,b,c)}\ast w>0
and at least one among
(a,b)
and
(b,c)
does not belong to
R^{\mathbf{A}}
. As shown above, this leads to a contradiction. It follows that

E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast q_{/\pi_{\mathbf{j}}}=E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})
, which proves the claim.

To show that
\vartheta
preserves
R_{k}
, we shall prove that the consistency equation (
5
) holds, and then apply Proposition
9
. Take
\mathbf{x}\in(R^{\mathbf{X}})^{k}
,
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[k]^{k}
, and
\mathbf{a}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}
. Consider also the tuple
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{2k})\in[2k]^{2k}
defined by
j_{2\ell-1}=2i_{\ell}-1
,
j_{2\ell}=2i_{\ell}
for
\ell\in[k]
. We find

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x})&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{b}\in(R^{\mathbf{A}})^{k}\\
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{b}^{\prime}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})&\leq&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{c}\in A^{2k}\\
\mathbf{c}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})\\[25.0pt]
=&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\Pi_{\mathbf{j}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})&=&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi((\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{\mathbf{j}})&=&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\ast\xi((\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})^{\prime})&=&\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{a}}\ast\vartheta(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}}),\end{array}

where the first and third equalities follow from Lemma
17
, the fourth from Proposition
9
applied to
\xi
, the fifth from the fact that
(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})_{\mathbf{j}}=(\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i}})^{\prime}
, and the inequality from the fact that
\mathbf{b}_{\mathbf{i}}=\mathbf{a}
if and only if
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}_{\mathbf{j}}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
. If strict inequality hold in (
6
), there would exist some
\mathbf{c}\in A^{2k}
such that
E_{\mathbf{c}}\ast\xi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})>0
and
(c_{2\ell-1},c_{2\ell})\not\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
for some
\ell\in[k]
– which would contradict Proposition
26
. It follows that
\vartheta
satisfies the consistency equation, and is thus a homomorphism.
∎

We have now all the ingredients to strengthen Remark
32

and prove that the approximate graph colouring problem is not solved by any constant level of Sherali-Adams.

Proof of Theorem
10
.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
\SA^{k}
solves
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
for some
3\leq c\leq d
. Since
\SA^{2}
is at least as strong as
\SA^{1}
, we can assume
k\geq 2
. We will now use Theorem
34
and Proposition
12
to derive a contradiction to Proposition
11
.

If
c\geq 4
, we can take
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
b^{(i)}(c)\geq k\cdot 2^{i}
. Consider the graph
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{K}_{a^{(i)}(d)+1}
and observe that, by Proposition
11
(cf. Remark
32
),
\SA^{k\cdot 2^{i}}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{b^{(i)}(c)})
accepts. Applying Proposition
12
repeatedly, we find that
\SA^{k}(\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{X},\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{K}_{b^{(i)}(c)})
accepts, where
\delta^{(i)}
denotes the application of the line digraph operator
i
-many times. Indicating by
\sim
the
k
-enhanced completion of a structure, Theorem
7
then yields

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{X}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}((\widetilde{\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{K}_{b^{(i)}(c)}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}).

Applying the second part of Theorem
34
repeatedly, we find
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{K}_{b^{(i)}(c)}\to\mathbf{K}_{c}
. In combination with Lemma
19
and Lemma
41
in Appendix
B
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}((\widetilde{\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{K}_{b^{(i)}(c)}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})

so that, composing the two homomorphisms, we obtain

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{X}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{c}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}).

It follows that
\SA^{k}(\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{c})
accepts; since we are supposing that
\SA^{k}
solves
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{c},\mathbf{K}_{d})
, this yields
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{d}
. Applying the first part of Theorem
34
repeatedly, we find
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{a^{(i)}(d)}
, a contradiction.

If
c=3
, we use the fact, observed in
[
74
, Lemma 4.19]
, that
\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{K}_{4}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
. Let
\mathbf{X}
be a digraph such that
\SA^{4k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{4})
accepts. From Proposition
12
, we find that
\SA^{k}(\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{X},\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{K}_{4})
accepts. Reasoning as above, we obtain

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{X}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}((\widetilde{\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{K}_{4}})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\tilde{\mathbf{K}}_{3}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}),

so
\SA^{k}(\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{X},\mathbf{K}_{3})
accepts, whence we get
\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{d}
and, from the first part of Theorem
34
,
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{K}_{a^{(2)}(d)}
. This means that
\SA^{4k}
solves
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{4},\mathbf{K}_{a^{(2)}(d)})
, which yields a contradiction as discussed above.
∎

7
Acceptance vs. solvability

In light of the acceptance characterisation obtained in Theorem
7
,
one may be tempted to conjecture that
\SA^{k}
solves a
\PCSP
template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
if and only if there exists a minion homomorphism
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
. The latter condition is certainly sufficient. Indeed, it follows from
[
10
, Lemma 4.4]
that
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is equivalent to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
. If this is the case, Proposition
22
implies that any
\sigma
-structure
\mathbf{X}
such that
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts satisfies
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
. By virtue of Lemma
19
, this yields
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{B}
. Also, clearly, if
\SA^{k}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
does not accept then
\mathbf{X}\not\to\mathbf{A}
.

However,
for
k\geq 2
the condition
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is far from being necessary. A consequence of Theorem
37
, which we shall prove in this section, is that
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is in fact equivalent to
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
; this last condition, in turn, is equivalent to
\BLP
solving
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
(cf. Theorem
39
in Appendix
A
). Since
\SA^{k}
is strictly stronger than
\BLP
(for example, when applied to
\CSP
s), we deduce that
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
is not necessary for
\SA^{k}
to solve
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
.

We start off with two technical results – Proposition
35
and Lemma
36
– both needed in the proof of Theorem
37
.

An
isomorphism
between two
\sigma
-structures (or two minions) is a bijective homomorphism whose inverse is also a homomorphism.

Proposition 35
.

Let
L,k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathbf{A}
be a
\sigma
-structure. Then there exists an isomorphism
\xi:{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}
.

Proof.

Consider the map
\xi:{(A^{L})}^{k}\to{(A^{k})}^{L}
defined as follows: Given
\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\in A^{L}
, let
M\in A^{L\times k}
be the matrix satisfying
M\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}

\forall i\in[k]
, and set
\xi((\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{k}))\coloneqq(M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L})
. Clearly,
\xi
is bijective. We now show that
\xi
is a homomorphism from
{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}
and
\xi^{-1}
is a homomorphism from
{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}
to
{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
.

To prove that
\xi
is a homomorphism, choose
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, let
\tau=(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}^{L}}
, where
\tau_{i}\in A^{L}

\forall i\in[r]
, and consider
\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
. Let
Q_{\tau}\in A^{L\times r}
be the matrix satisfying
Q_{\tau}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\tau_{i}

\forall i\in[r]
. By the definition of the
L
-th power of a relational structure,
Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}

\forall j\in[L]
; hence,
(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}

\forall j\in[L]
. Consider now the tensor
\varphi\in\mathcal{T}^{k;r}((A^{k})^{L})
defined by
E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\coloneqq\left((Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1})_{\mathbf{i}},\dots,(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L})_{\mathbf{i}}\right)

\forall\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}
. Observe that, for any
j\in[L]
,
\left[(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j})_{\mathbf{i}}\right]_{\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}}=(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j})^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
, so
\varphi\in R^{{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}}
. We claim that
\xi(\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})=\varphi
; this would imply that
\xi
is a homomorphism. Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[r]^{k}
, and consider the matrix
K\in\{0,1\}^{r\times k}
satisfying
K\mathbf{e}_{j}=\mathbf{e}_{i_{j}}

\forall j\in[k]
. We find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\xi\left(\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\xi\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)=\xi(\tau_{\mathbf{i}})=\xi((\tau_{i_{1}},\dots,\tau_{i_{k}}))

\displaystyle=\xi\left(\left(Q_{\tau}\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}},\dots,Q_{\tau}\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi\left(\left(Q_{\tau}K\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,Q_{\tau}K\mathbf{e}_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(K^{T}Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,K^{T}Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1}\right)_{\mathbf{i}},\dots,\left(Q_{\tau}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L}\right)_{\mathbf{i}}\right)=E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi,

from which the claim follows.

To prove that
\xi^{-1}
is a homomorphism, choose
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and take
\varphi\in R^{{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}}
– that is,
\varphi
is a tensor in
\mathcal{T}^{k;r}((A^{k})^{L})
such that, for each
j\in[L]
,

\varphi_{(j)}\coloneqq\left[\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\right)_{j}\right]_{\mathbf{i}\in[r]^{k}}\in R^{\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}},

where
\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\right)_{j}\in A^{k}
is the
j
-th element in the tuple
E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\in(A^{k})^{L}
. Therefore, for each
j\in[L]
,
\varphi_{(j)}=\mathbf{a}_{(j)}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
for some
\mathbf{a}_{(j)}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. Consider the matrix
Q\in A^{L\times r}
satisfying
Q^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\mathbf{a}_{(j)}

\forall j\in[L]
, and observe that
\tau\coloneqq\left(Q\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,Q\mathbf{e}_{r}\right)\in R^{\mathbf{A}^{L}}
, so
\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\in R^{{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}}
. We claim that
\xi^{-1}(\varphi)=\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
; this would imply that
\xi^{-1}
is a homomorphism. Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})\in[r]^{k}
, and consider the matrix
K
introduced above. We find

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\xi^{-1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi)=\xi^{-1}\left(\left(\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\right)_{1},\dots,\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi\right)_{L}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi_{(1)},\dots,E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\varphi_{(L)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left(E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{a}_{(1)}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\dots,E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\mathbf{a}_{(L)}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left((\mathbf{a}_{(1)})_{\mathbf{i}},\dots,(\mathbf{a}_{(L)})_{\mathbf{i}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left(K^{T}\mathbf{a}_{(1)},\dots,K^{T}\mathbf{a}_{(L)}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left(K^{T}Q^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,K^{T}Q^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi^{-1}\left(\left((QK)^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,(QK)^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(QK\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,QK\mathbf{e}_{k}\right)=\left(Q\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}},\dots,Q\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau_{\mathbf{i}}=E_{\mathbf{i}}\ast\tau^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},

from which the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 36
.

Let
k,L\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathbf{A}
be a
k
-enhanced
\sigma
-structure. Then
\mathbf{A}^{L}
is
k
-enhanced, too.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{A}
is
k
-enhanced,
\sigma
contains a
k
-ary symbol
R_{k}
such that
R_{k}^{\mathbf{A}}=A^{k}
. We claim that
R_{k}^{\mathbf{A}^{L}}=(A^{L})^{k}
. Clearly, we have
R_{k}^{\mathbf{A}^{L}}\subseteq(A^{L})^{k}
. To prove the other inclusion, consider
k
tuples
\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\in A^{L}
, and let
M
be the matrix in
A^{L\times k}
satisfying
M\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}
for
i\in[k]
. Note that, for each
j\in[L]
,
M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{j}\in A^{k}=R_{k}^{\mathbf{A}}
. Hence, by the definition of the
L
-th power of a relational structure,
(\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{k})\in R_{k}^{\mathbf{A}^{L}}
. This concludes the proof of the claim and of the lemma.
∎

We say that two minions
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
are
homomorphically equivalent
if
\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{N}
and
\mathscr{N}\to\mathscr{M}
.

Theorem 37
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
be a
\PCSP
template. Then
\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
are homomorphically equivalent; if, in addition,
\mathbf{A}
is
k
-enhanced, then
\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
are isomorphic.

Proof.

For
L\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the maps

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\rho_{L}:&\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{L},\mathbf{B})&\to&\Hom\left({(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right),\\
&f&\mapsto&f^{\ast}\\[5.0pt]
\rho^{\prime}_{L}:&\Hom\left({(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)&\to&\Hom(\mathbf{A}^{L},\mathbf{B})\\
&g&\mapsto&g_{\ast}\end{array}

introduced in the proof of Lemma
19
. In addition, consider the isomorphism
\xi_{L}:{(\mathbf{A}^{L})}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\to{\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)}^{L}
described in Proposition
35
. We define the map
\psi:\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
by setting
f\mapsto\rho_{L}(f)\circ\xi_{L}^{-1}
for
f\in\left(\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})\right)^{(L)}
. Moreover, we define the map
\psi^{\prime}:\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\to\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
by setting
g\mapsto\rho^{\prime}_{L}(g\circ\xi_{L})
for
g\in\left(\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})\right)^{(L)}
. Clearly, both
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
preserve the arities. We now show that they also preserve the minors. Choose a map
\pi:[L]\to[L^{\prime}]
for some
L,L^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
{P}
be the matrix in
\{0,1\}^{L^{\prime}\times L}
satisfying
{P}\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{e}_{\pi(i)}
for
i\in[L]
.

Consider a map
f:\mathbf{A}^{L}\to\mathbf{B}
and
L^{\prime}
tuples
\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{L^{\prime}}\in A^{k}
, and let
M\in A^{k\times L^{\prime}}
be the matrix satisfying
M\mathbf{e}_{i}=\mathbf{a}_{i}
for
i\in[L^{\prime}]
. Observe that

\displaystyle\psi(f)_{/\pi}((\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{L^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\psi(f)_{/\pi}((M\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M\mathbf{e}_{L^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\psi(f)((M\mathbf{e}_{\pi(1)},\dots,M\mathbf{e}_{\pi(L)}))

\displaystyle=\psi(f)((M{P}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M{P}\mathbf{e}_{L}))

\displaystyle=\rho_{L}(f)\left(\xi_{L}^{-1}((M{P}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M{P}\mathbf{e}_{L}))\right)

\displaystyle=\rho_{L}(f)(({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,{P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k}))

\displaystyle=f^{*}(({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,{P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k}))

\displaystyle=(f({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1}),\dots,f({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k})),

\displaystyle\psi(f_{/\pi})((\mathbf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}_{L^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\psi(f_{/\pi})((M\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M\mathbf{e}_{L^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\left[\rho_{L^{\prime}}(f_{/\pi})\right]\left(\xi_{L^{\prime}}^{-1}((M\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M\mathbf{e}_{L^{\prime}}))\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\rho_{L^{\prime}}(f_{/\pi})\right]((M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k}))

\displaystyle=(f_{/\pi})^{*}((M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k}))

\displaystyle=\left(f_{/\pi}(M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1}),\dots,f_{/\pi}(M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=(f({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1}),\dots,f({P}^{T}M^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k})),

so
\psi(f)_{/\pi}=\psi(f_{/\pi})
. Hence,
\psi
is a minion homomorphism.

Consider now a map
g:\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)^{L}\to\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
and a tuple
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{L^{\prime}})\in A^{L^{\prime}}
. We find

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(g)_{/\pi}(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime}(g)({P}^{T}\mathbf{a})=[\rho^{\prime}_{L}(g\circ\xi_{L})]({P}^{T}\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=(g\circ\xi_{L})_{*}({P}^{T}\mathbf{a})=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}[(g\circ\xi_{L})(({P}^{T}\mathbf{a},\dots,{P}^{T}\mathbf{a}))]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}[g(\xi_{L}(({P}^{T}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{1}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,{P}^{T}\mathbf{a}\mathbf{1}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k})))]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}[g((\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}{P}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}{P}\mathbf{e}_{L}))],

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(g_{/\pi})(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=[\rho^{\prime}_{L^{\prime}}(g_{/\pi}\circ\xi_{L^{\prime}})](\mathbf{a})=(g_{/\pi}\circ\xi_{L^{\prime}})_{*}(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\left[(g_{/\pi}\circ\xi_{L^{\prime}})((\mathbf{a},\dots,\mathbf{a}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\left[g_{/\pi}(\xi_{L^{\prime}}((\mathbf{a}\mathbf{1}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{a}\mathbf{1}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{k})))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\left[g_{/\pi}((\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{L^{\prime}}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\left[g((\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{\pi(1)},\dots,\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{\pi(L)}))\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\left[g((\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}{P}\mathbf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{1}_{k}\mathbf{a}^{T}{P}\mathbf{e}_{L}))\right],

so
\psi^{\prime}(g)_{/\pi}=\psi^{\prime}(g_{/\pi})
. Hence,
\psi^{\prime}
is a minion homomorphism, too. This concludes the proof that
\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
are homomorphically equivalent.

Suppose now that
\mathbf{A}
is
k
-enhanced. By virtue of Lemma
36
,
\mathbf{A}^{L}
is
k
-enhanced for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
. Using part
(ii)
of Lemma
19
, we deduce that, in this case, the maps
\rho_{L}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{L}
are mutually inverse bijections. Therefore, given
f:\mathbf{A}^{L}\to\mathbf{B}
and
g:\left(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}\right)^{L}\to\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
, we find

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllllllll}(\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi)(f)&=&\psi^{\prime}(\rho_{L}(f)\circ\xi_{L}^{-1})&=&\rho^{\prime}_{L}(\rho_{L}(f)\circ\xi_{L}^{-1}\circ\xi_{L})&=&\rho^{\prime}_{L}(\rho_{L}(f))&=&f,\\[7.0pt]
(\psi\circ\psi^{\prime})(g)&=&\psi(\rho^{\prime}_{L}(g\circ\xi_{L}))&=&\rho_{L}(\rho^{\prime}_{L}(g\circ\xi_{L}))\circ\xi_{L}^{-1}&=&g\circ\xi_{L}\circ\xi_{L}^{-1}&=&g.\end{array}

Hence,
\psi
and
\psi^{\prime}
are mutually inverse minion homomorphisms, so
\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
and
\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
are isomorphic.
∎

As seen at the beginning of the section, a consequence of Theorem
37
is that the condition
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}\to\Pol(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}},\mathbf{B}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
does not characterise solvability of a
\PCSP
template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
through
\SA^{k}
and, in fact, it is equivalent to solvability through
\BLP
. In the next, final remark we argue that the theory of
tensor rank
(cf.
[
78
]
) could play a role in understanding the power of Sherali-Adams.

Remark 38
.

One primary message of this work is that the multilinear structure
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
determines acceptance for the
k
-th level of Sherali-Adams in the same way as the linear structure

\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
determines acceptance for
\BLP
. What is the connection between these two structures? We recall from Proposition
24
that there exists a natural homomorphism
\psi
from
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
to
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
. We have also seen that
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
carries the same information as
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
(in the sense of Lemma
19
); indeed, the problem of understanding whether an input structure
\mathbf{X}
is homomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
is equivalent to the problem of understanding whether
\mathbf{X}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
is homomorphic to
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
. Now, intuitively speaking, the relations in
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
should be seen as sets of tensors having generic rank, while the relations in
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
are sets of rank-one tensors obtained as outer Segre products of vectors (cf. Footnote
3
). Then, the homomorphism
\psi
in the proof of Proposition
24
can be viewed as a
compressive-sensing
procedure taking a tensor
T
as input and returning an approximation of
T
having rank one as output. In other words, the structure corresponding to
\BLP
is essentially a rank-one compression of the structure corresponding to
\SA^{k}
. Reconstructing the signal from a rank-one compression is impossible in general, so we do not have a homomorphism from
[\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})]^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
for
k\geq 2
– which corresponds to the fact that Sherali-Adams is strictly more powerful than
\BLP
.

This informal argument suggests a strategy to characterise the power of the Sherali-Adams hierarchy in terms of the (known) characterisation for
\BLP
by studying the possible ways to decompose the tensors in
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A}^{\textsuperscript{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.1pt}{\tiny k}}}}})
in combinations of rank-one components – i.e., the so-called
rank-retaining decompositions
of the tensors (for example, see
[
75
]
).

Appendix A
BLP relaxation

Let
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{A}
be
\sigma
-structures. We introduce a variable
\lambda_{x}(a)
for every
x\in X
,
a\in A
, and a variable
\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{a})
for every
R\in\sigma,\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}},\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. The system

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\lambda_{x}(a)&=1&x\in X\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}\\
a_{i}=a\end{subarray}}\lambda_{R,\mathbf{x}}(\mathbf{a})&=\lambda_{x_{i}}(a)&R\in\sigma,\mathbf{x}\in R^{\mathbf{X}},a\in A,i\in[\ar(R)]\end{array}

admits a
0
–
1
solution exactly when
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{A}
. If the variables are allowed to take values in the interval
[0,1]
, we can check the existence of a solution to (
A
) in polynomial time (in the size of
\mathbf{X}
). This yields the so-called basic linear programming (
\BLP
) relaxation
[
76
]
: We say that
\BLP(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})

accepts
if (
A
) has a solution such that all variables take real (equivalently, rational) values in
[0,1]
. From
[
10
]
, this is equivalent to the existence of a homomorphism
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}}(\mathbf{A})
.

By construction, if
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{A}
then

\BLP(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts. We say that
\BLP

solves
a
\PCSP
template
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
if whenever
\BLP(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{A})
accepts we have
\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{B}
.
Solvability of
\PCSP
s (and, thus,
\CSP
s) through
\BLP
is characterised algebraically in the following result.

Theorem 39

(
[
10
]
)
.

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
be a
\PCSP
template. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\BLP
solves
\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
;

(2)

\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
admits a minion homomorphism from
\mathscr{Q}_{\operatorname{conv}}
;

(3)

\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
contains symmetric operations
11
11

11

An
L
-ary operation
f:A^{L}\to B
is called
symmetric
if

f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{L})=f(a_{\pi(1)},\ldots,a_{\pi(L)})
for every
a_{1},\ldots,a_{L}\in A
and every permutation
\pi:[L]\to[L]
.

of all arities.

Appendix B
Two facts about minions

We present two simple facts about minions. The first is implicitly proved in
[
10
]
for the special case of minions of operations.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a nonempty minion and let
\mathbf{A}
be a
\sigma
-structure. Then
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{M}
is nonempty, it contains a unary element
M\in\mathscr{M}^{(1)}
. Consider the map

\displaystyle f:A

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{M}^{(n)}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto M_{/\rho_{a}}

where
\rho_{a}:[1]\to[n]=A
is defined by
\rho_{a}(1)=a
. Take
R\in\sigma
of arity
r
, and consider a tuple
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. Let
m=|R^{\mathbf{A}}|
, and consider the function
\pi:[1]\to[m]
defined by
\pi(1)=\mathbf{a}
. Let
q=M_{/\pi}\in\mathscr{M}^{(m)}
. For each
i\in[r]
, consider the function
\pi_{i}:[m]\to[n]
defined by
\pi_{i}(\mathbf{b})=b_{i}
, where
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. Observe that
\rho_{a_{i}}=\pi_{i}\circ\pi
for each
i\in[r]
. We obtain

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{a})

\displaystyle=(f(a_{1}),\dots,f(a_{r}))=(M_{/\rho_{a_{1}}},\dots,M_{/\rho_{a_{r}}})=(M_{/\pi_{1}\circ\pi},\dots,M_{/\pi_{r}\circ\pi})

\displaystyle=((M_{/\pi})_{/\pi_{1}},\dots,(M_{/\pi})_{/\pi_{r}})=(q_{/\pi_{1}},\dots,q_{/\pi_{r}})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})},

thus showing that
f
is a homomorphism from
\mathbf{A}
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})
.
∎

Lemma 41
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion and let
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B}
be
\sigma
-structures with
\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
. Then
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})\to\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{B})
.

Proof.

Fix a homomorphism
f:\mathbf{A}\to\mathbf{B}
, and consider the map
\xi:\mathscr{M}^{(|A|)}\to\mathscr{M}^{(|B|)}
defined by
M\mapsto M_{/f}
for
M\in\mathscr{M}^{(|A|)}
. We need to show that
\xi
yields a homomorphism from
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})
to
\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{B})
. Let
R\in\sigma
be a relation symbol of arity
r
, and take a tuple
(M_{1},\dots,M_{r})\in R^{\mathbb{F}_{\mathscr{M}}(\mathbf{A})}
. This means that there exists
q\in\mathscr{M}^{(|R^{\mathbf{A}}|)}
such that
M_{i}=q_{/\pi_{i}}
for each
i\in[r]
, where
\pi_{i}:R^{\mathbf{A}}\to A
is defined by
\mathbf{a}\mapsto a_{i}
for
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
. We have that
\xi((M_{1},\dots,M_{r}))=({M_{1}}_{/f},\dots,{M_{r}}_{/f})
. Consider the map
g:R^{\mathbf{A}}\to R^{\mathbf{B}}
defined by
\mathbf{a}\mapsto f(\mathbf{a})
for
\mathbf{a}\in R^{\mathbf{A}}
, and take
\tilde{q}=q_{/g}\in\mathscr{M}^{(|R^{\mathbf{B}}|)}
. We claim that
{M_{i}}_{/f}=\tilde{q}_{/\tilde{\pi}_{i}}
for
i\in[r]
, where
\tilde{\pi}_{i}:R^{\mathbf{B}}\to B
is defined by
\mathbf{b}\mapsto b_{i}
for
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{r})\in R^{\mathbf{B}}
. This would conclude the proof of the lemma. Observe that
{M_{i}}_{/f}=(q_{/\pi_{i}})_{/f}=q_{/f\circ\pi_{i}}
and
\tilde{q}_{/\tilde{\pi}_{i}}=(q_{/g})_{/\tilde{\pi}_{i}}=q_{/\tilde{\pi}_{i}\circ g}
. The claim follows since
f\circ\pi_{i}=\tilde{\pi}_{i}\circ g
.
∎
